### Page 10

TO P I C  4 | EL EC TR I F I C ATI O N
Türkiye is championing a voluntary pledge to lift electrification from 23% to 35% by 2035
Electrification is expected to be at the centre of this “implementation COP”, targeting a rise in electricity’s share of global final energy use from 23% to 35% by 2035. We expect support for this
action given it is being positioned alongside energy security, but we also expect discussion and debate over how it is delivered (e.g., the role of renewables, nuclear and natural gas), alongside

concerns over clean tech dependency in supply chains. China and the EU have expressed support but not yet committed.
Some may brand this the electrification COP
The Action Agenda pledge aims to raise electricity's share of global final energy from 23%
today to 35% by 2035. The pledge was launched in Bonn this past June and formalised as the
"35-by-35 Global Electrification Pledge" in New York on 21 September.
China and the EU have signaled support, but have not yet signed the pledge

Electrification fits modern day energy-security goals making it a viable target for this year’s
COP. China’s cleantech dominance means it has a further incentive to support initiatives that
seek to increase the pace of global electrification. It also aligns closely with China's existing
development trajectory. While the 35% goal is based on Paris-aligned pathways supported by
IEA and IRENA analysis, the numerical target itself does not require the additional electricity to

come from non fossil fuel sources. This has invited some criticism, though it may make it
easier for some nations to sign the non-binding pledge.
Alignment with the voluntary renewables pledge
Recall that COP28 called for tripling global renewable power capacity by 2030 in COP28’s
formal Global Stocktake decision, equivalent to around 11.2 TW. IRENA’s September 2026
assessment puts capacity at 5.15 TW at end-2025. Record additions of 693 GW in 2025 remain

below the 1,200 GW needed annually in 2026–30. The 35-by-35 pledge complements this
supply goal by shifting transport, buildings and industry towards electricity, enabling
renewable power to replace fossil fuels. Its emissions benefit still depends on cleaner
generation.
We view the goal positively, but delivery needs grid enhancements.
IRENA counts ~2,500 GW of renewable, storage and large-load projects awaiting connection
and says annual investment to strengthen grid infrastructure and enhance system flexibility

must rise from ~$500bn in 2025 to around $1tn through 2035, and $1.2tn thereafter. That
said, signatories signal support, but domestic delivery plans will determine investment.
Sources: UNFCCC, COP31 Presidency, IEA, IRENA

TO P I C  5   |   F O S S I L  F U EL S
The fossil fuel transition roadmap track is growing outside the formal COP process
COP30 failed to agree a fossil fuel transition roadmap, despite support from more than 80 countries. Brazil’s COP30 presidency is therefore developing a roadmap outside formal UN
negotiations to be presented ahead of COP31. Separately, Colombia and the Netherlands convened a ‘coalition of the willing’ of 57 countries in Santa Marta in April 2026 to explore practical

steps towards a fossil fuel phase-out. This parallel forum allows willing countries to cooperate without needing consensus from all COP parties.
Sources: Carbon Brief, Santa Marta conference report

TO P I C  6 | TR AD E
Trade could resurface at COP31 as a challenge to collective agreement; talks have been requested
Trade tensions could feature at COP31 as governments weigh climate ambition against industrial competitiveness. Developing countries fear that measures such as the CBAM disadvantage

their exporters, while the EU says they prevent carbon leakage. COP30 agreed three annual dialogues on trade's role in climate cooperation (no binding outcome), creating for the first time a
formal space for trade discussions in the UNFCCC process, but The African Group and others would like it on the formal COP31 agenda.
Do trade discussions belong at COP?
Developing countries have long pushed back against climate-related trade measures such as
the EU's Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM) and Deforestation Regulation (EUDR).

They argue such measures are protectionism disguised as climate policy and
disproportionately impact poorer countries. Developed countries view such measures as
necessary to level the playing field for domestic firms in cases where foreign competitors face
lower environment-related compliance costs relative to domestic industries, while also arguing
that COP is not the appropriate forum to discuss trade matters. Despite this, a European
Parliament briefing noted trade is a point of “exposure” for the EU at COP31.

COP30’s agreement on trade dialogue was notable, but it may not be enough
COP30 agreed three annual climate-trade dialogues at the mid-year sessions in 2026-28, with
a high-level event in 2028. The first, in Bonn (13 June), was calmer than expected but exposed
the divide: developing countries argued that climate-linked trade measures such as CBAM
raise costs and restrict market access and called for impact assessments within the UNFCCC,
while the EU defended them as responses to environmental externalities. With trade also

raised in other Bonn rooms, it is far from clear the dialogue will contain the issue in Antalya.
The African Group of negotiators has requested a Trade dialogue on the formal agenda.
China has already said it intends to use COP31 to oppose cleantech trade barriers, with its vice
minister of the Ministry of Ecology and Environment saying the event should discuss “in depth”
the “new problem of unilateralism and protectionism undermining global collective efforts” on

climate. The African Group is also pushing for trade to be on the formal agenda. Ultimately,
trade talks could prove more consequential at COP31 than in previous years given inflationary
pressures and concerns over business competitiveness, but it is hard to assess the potential
impact other than possibly causing delays in agreement. However, even if the impact is
limited, the issue could shape negotiations elsewhere outside of the COP process.
Sources: European Parliament, Carbon Brief, Reuters

### Page 13

TOP I C 7 | G EO P O L I TI CS  & MU L TI L ATER AL I S M (1  o f 2 )
Multilateralism is at a crossroads
Geopolitical fragmentation and the US exit from the Paris Agreement make consensus among nearly 200 nations harder, encouraging “coalitions of the willing” (outside of the formal COP
negotiations) to pursue more ambitious climate goals. COP31’s success or failure in securing multilateral agreement or credible voluntary pledges could shape future COPs by showing which

approaches deliver progress. The US retreat from the Paris Agreement will also continue to have far-reaching implications for climate finance. Overall, the EU and China will be expected to lead.
The consensus agreement principle under strain
The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) provides the
multilateral framework for COP, the forum where countries negotiate collective climate
decisions. The process is anchored by the view that all countries must act on climate change,

but the extent of their efforts depends on national circumstances. This puts the onus on
developed countries to lead. The question is whether this principle still holds against a more
fragmented backdrop and transactional diplomatic context as national budgets prioritize
security and business competitiveness. With the COP process still separating developed and
developing countries by their 1992 UNFCCC classifications, developed countries are continuing

to push for wealthier developing nations such as China and Saudi Arabia to play a greater role
on finance. This has been a major point of contention at recent summits.
The US stepping back has far-reaching implications
The US skipped COP30 and left the Paris Agreement. It will formally exit the UNFCCC in
February 2027. This retreat has weighed on broader climate-finance decisions, both from a
monetary sense and through the loss of negotiating power. While cities, states and businesses

from the US continue to support COP efforts (e.g., groups like America Is All In), the federal
administration’s absence has put more pressure on the EU and China to lead.
What does an evolving process look like?
Because formal COP decisions require consensus (or lack of objection), progress often stalls or
moves slowly. COP30 saw Brazil move items where there was strong, but not unanimous,
support such as the fossil fuel roadmap, to separate streams outside the formal COP process.

Countries disappointed with the lack of stronger mitigation action also launched a separate
summit on transitioning away from fossil fuels, held this past April in Colombia. While COP is
the flagship global forum, these actions demonstrate a shift towards “coalitions of the willing”
for issues where global agreement is not possible.
Sources: EU Council, EU Parliament, Carbon Brief, UN, WRI

TO P I C  7 | G EO P O L I TI C S  &  MU L TI L ATER AL I S M (2  o f 2 )
Much will rest on the approach of the EU and China

With the US absent, the EU and China are the two actors with the greatest diplomatic and economic leverage with which to broker compromise agreements between the different factions at
COP31. This will require careful diplomacy, with their interests not fully aligned in some areas; discussions around trade and climate finance may be particularly challenging. At the same time,
both have reiterated their continued support of climate action and multilateral decision making; a successful COP outcome would back up these commitments.

### Page 15

TO P I C  8 | AI  &  C L I MATE
The Antalya AI Pledge puts data-centre power demand on the agenda
We expect AI’s climate potential and rising electricity demand to make it a prominent COP31 topic, prompting debate over COPs’ role in data-centre energy use. The Antalya AI Pledge invites
governments and companies to commit voluntarily to sustainable infrastructure and environmental transparency. Its call for zero- and low-emissions energy infrastructure leaves room for

nuclear power, prompting discussion of its role in meeting data-centre demand alongside renewables.
The Antalya AI Pledge seeks to build stakeholder support for sustainable AI buildout
Türkiye is encouraging stakeholders (inc. corporates) to commit to the pledge, asking
signatories to “declare our shared ambition to make AI part of the climate solution. This
means sustainably powered, efficiently designed, transparently measured and available to all”.

Signatories will commit to: 1) Power and build AI infrastructure and hardware sustainably, 2)
Design, manage or procure AI responsibly across its lifecycle, 3) Support assessment and
reporting of the environmental impacts of AI systems, 4) Strengthen financial support and
technical infrastructure for AI for climate action.
The AI for Clean Technologies Initiative seeks to highlight AI climate solutions
The initiative plans to develop AI-enabled pilot projects (e.g. smart energy systems) and then

link the projects with industry partners and investors, aiming to demonstrate AI’s potential to
contribute to sustainability goals. For grids, the IEA sees AI improving forecasting, congestion
management and outage response, helping operators get more from existing lines.
The dialogue here will set the stage for AI discussions at future COPs
While the pledge is voluntary, many of these themes are already beginning to appear in early-
stage regulatory demands, with transparency and disclosure receiving particular focus. There

will be questions over how effective the pledge can be given the US has disengaged from the
COP process, though California could send a strong signal if it commits to the pledge. We will
be particularly focused on how the tech industry responds. Despite the trend of greenhushing,
we expect many to send delegations to Antalya, with AI companies and investors viewing
responsible practices and community engagement as key to avoiding backlash and delays to

AI buildout. Note that responsible AI was also a hot topic at the Principles for Responsible
Investment conference this year in October.
Sources: COP31 Presidency, IEA

### Page 16

TO P I C  9 | N ATU R E &  C AR B O N  MAR K ETS
Nature and carbon markets have key roles to play in supporting climate action
Carbon credit discussions will centre on government policies to boost demand and corporate engagement, with much of the formal UN architecture now in place. If carbon markets can scale,
the benefits would be wide-ranging (e.g. supporting emissions targets and providing finance for developing countries). Credibility and greenwashing concerns remain key risks however; we

expect prices to diverge in favour of high-integrity credits. The Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF) now has ~$7.8bn of sponsor capital against a $10bn end-2026 target.
Sources: Tropical Forest Forever Facility, Coalition to Grow Carbon Markets
16
Voluntary carbon market development
Türkiye has stated a focus on carbon markets at COP31, particularly on government actions to
boost demand, highlighting the potential for carbon markets to unlock more than $50bn in

additional annual finance for developing nations. The Coalition to Grow Carbon Markets
(made up of 13 governments) has announced it will unveil a “Policy Playbook” at COP31 to
support market development. While much of Article 6 (carbon credits under UN process) has
moved to delivery, countries will aim to reach agreement on the funding model for Article 6.2
at COP31. Further carbon market progress stands to have multiple benefits; it can support
developed countries in hitting climate goals while reducing immediate decarbonisation

requirements, while also providing finance and supporting ecosystems in developing ones.
Understanding the pushback
Carbon markets have seen pushback over credit integrity (including regulatory scrutiny) and
the risk that offsets delay domestic cuts. UN involvement is needed to enforce common
accounting and integrity rules, such as the Article 6 rulebook finalised at COP29 and due for
review in 2028, that stop the same reduction being claimed twice. Without that backstop,

fragmented bilateral and voluntary schemes risk eroding the trust needed to scale the market.
Update on the TFFF
The Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF), launched at COP30 last year, aims for $10bn of
sponsor contributions by year-end. It pays tropical-forest countries per hectare conserved,
discounts payments for each hectare lost or degraded, and directs at least 20% to Indigenous
Peoples and local communities. Its Charter was adopted on 22 July 2026 and received a

tailwind following the re-election of Brazilian President Lula, who is a key supporter. Shortly
after Lula’s re-election, the Netherlands pledged up to $500mn to the fund, bringing the total
pledged to $7.8bn. Further financing commitments are key, with Norway’s $3bn contribution
conditional on the fund reaching at least $10bn by the end of 2026.

TO P I C  1 0 | P AC I F I C , O C EANS  & S EA L EV EL  R I S E
Oceans will be a priority item, with Australia promising to champion Pacific interests
Australia’s ask for ceding the hosting was a Pacific pre-COP, and it has promised to continue championing Pacific interests at COP31. The Taku Pakasoa Declaration, signed at the pre-COP,

reflects Pacific priorities, calling for faster emissions cuts, more adaptation finance and financial system reform. The climate-ocean nexus will also be a focus, including investable solutions.
Championing Pacific interests is a key focus for Australia
As part of the hosting compromise, Australia co-hosted the official pre-COP with Fiji and
Tuvalu (5–8 Oct), attended by countries including Germany, the UAE and India (though some
questioned the low number of international leaders who attended). Australia has pledged to

keep championing Pacific interests as President of the Negotiations.
Taku Pakasoa Declaration reflects Pacific Priorities
Signed on 6 Oct at the pre-COP, it calls for faster emissions cuts, a substantial increase in
climate finance (particularly for adaptation in capacity-constrained countries), reform of the
international financial architecture, and for elevating the ocean-climate nexus. It also
welcomes the 35% electrification goal.
The challenges of rising sea levels are gaining increasing prominence

Sea levels in the region are rising faster than the global average, and half of Pacific Island
infrastructure lies within 500 metres of the coast. The UN General Assembly adopted the UN
Declaration on Sea Level Rise by consensus on 24 Sept; global sea levels rose nearly 6mm in
2024, the highest annual increase on record.
Oceans are key to climate stability, but more finance is needed
Pacific nations emphasise the critical role of oceans as a natural environmental regulator.

They absorb 30% of all carbon dioxide emissions (largest carbon sink) and capture 90% of the
excess heat generated by these emissions. Investor interest in sustainable ocean solutions is
growing, with 2026 YTD blue bond issuance already an annual record, yet bond issuance
supporting UN SDG 14: Life Below Water trails many other categories; scaling this up will be a
focus at COP31. A commitment to delivering “tangible, bankable ocean outcomes” at COP31 is

one of 13 points included as part of the Trabzon Ocean Call to Action unveiled by Türkiye,
while the ORRAA has unveiled a Blue Climate Finance Pathway.
Sources: Taku Pakasoa Declaration (UNFCCC), UN Declaration on Sea Level Rise

F U TU R E O F  C O P
Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, though questions over how COP will evolve are in focus
COP has shifted from setting targets to implementation. Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, where we expect a focus on delivering commitments and climate finance access, reflecting African
priorities. COP33 falls to Asia-Pacific in 2028, with China reportedly considering a bid and South Korea potentially interested. COP31–33 will test delivery in a fragmented world, with a second

Global Stocktake concluding in 2028. Its outcome should inform countries’ next national climate plans (NDCs, their emissions-reduction pledges), due in 2030.

AP P END I X  |   COP  TER MS
Appendix A: Key COP terms used in this report
This appendix explains the main COP process terms and negotiating concepts used throughout the report. Definitions are summarised for non-specialist readers and reflect how each term is
used in this deck.
Sources: UNFCCC; COP30 and COP31 Presidency documents; and the other sources referenced in this report
19
Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Preview  |  October 2026
TERM DEFINITION

Action Agenda Voluntary, non-binding pledges and initiatives launched alongside the formal talks. It is separate from negotiated decisions.
Annex II / Non-Annex I UNFCCC country classification. Annex II covers developed economies; Non-Annex I covers most other countries, including China and India.
Article 6 Paris Agreement framework for international carbon markets. Article 6.2 covers bilateral trading of carbon credits between countries.
Article 9

Paris Agreement finance provisions. Article 9.1 covers developed
countries’ obligation to provide finance. COP30 set a two-year work
programme for 2026-27.
Baku to Belém Roadmap
to $1.3T
Roadmap to scale climate finance to $1.3tn a year by 2035 from all
sources, inclusive of the $300bn NCQG.
Bonn sessions Mid-year technical talks of the UNFCCC Subsidiary Bodies (e.g. SB64) in Bonn, Germany, which prepare the ground for COP discussions.
Brazil fossil fuel roadmap COP30 Presidency’s voluntary roadmap for transitioning away from fossil fuels, pursued outside the formal UN negotiations.

Consensus How COP decisions are taken: adopted when no party formally objects, so a single party can theoretically block or dilute an outcome.
COP / COPs Conference of the Parties: the UNFCCC’s annual decision-making summit, where nearly 200 parties negotiate climate action.
COP Presidency The host country’s team that chairs the conference. For COP31, Türkiye’s Murat Kurum is COP President and leads the Action Agenda.
TERM DEFINITION
Final COP text Decisions adopted by consensus at the close of the COP, setting its formal outcome.

Formal agenda Negotiated items on the official COP agenda, decided by consensus. Separate from the voluntary Action Agenda.
Global Goal on
Adaptation (GGA)
Paris Agreement goal to strengthen resilience. COP30 adopted 59
indicators; the link to finance is contested.
Global Stocktake (GST) Five-yearly review of progress under the Paris Agreement. The second runs from COP31 to COP33 (2028).
Just transition
mechanism
Belém Action Mechanism agreed at COP30, focused on fairness in the

shift to a low-carbon economy.
Loss and damage fund Fund set up at COP27 and operationalised in 2023 to support vulnerable developing countries.
NCQG New Collective Quantified Goal (COP29): at least $300bn a year for developing countries by 2035, within a $1.3tn ambition.
NDC Nationally determined contribution: each country’s national climate plan, with emissions targets and actions, updated every five years.
Negotiating groups Blocs that coordinate national positions, such as the G77 & China, the EU, LDCs and AOSIS.

Paris Agreement 2015 treaty to hold warming well below 2°C and pursue 1.5°C, with national climate plans (NDCs) as its core mechanism.
Pre-COP Ministerial meeting ahead of the COP to test positions and build consensus. For COP31, Australia co-hosted a Pacific pre-COP.
President of Negotiations COP31 role held by Australia’s Chris Bowen, who drafts final texts and steers government-to-government talks.
UNFCCC UN Framework Convention on Climate Change: the 1992 treaty behind the climate process. Its parties meet annually at the COP.

Appendix C: The level of alarm over global warming has begun to move higher (again)
As part of our monthly Climate & Sustainability Survey, we ask consumers in the UK, US, Germany, France, Spain and Italy for their views on global warming. In Q3 2026, we recorded a notable
rebound in alarm regarding the pace of global warming, particularly in the UK and Europe following a summer of climate extremes. The level of alarm had begun to dip globally post the 2024 US

presidential election in November.

Document 349: Global Views_ Downside Growth Risks

Source PDF: all report pdf/Global Views_ Downside Growth Risks.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-10-26
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

Jan Hatzius 
+1(212)902-0394 | jan.hatzius@gs.com 
Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC
Global Views: Downside Growth Risks
26 October 2026 | 6:45AM EDT
1. So far in 2026, global growth has been remarkably unremarkable.  Both the world 
as a whole and the G3 economies are growing in line with our estimates of potential 
GDP.  Our interpretation is that fiscal easing and the positive impulse from the AI 
boom have offset the negative impulse from the war in Iran and the lingering drag 
from higher US tariffs.

2. Our standing global GDP forecasts project more of the same in 2027, as lower 
energy prices offset fading tailwinds from fiscal policy and AI.  But the balance of 
risks is on the downside.  The most immediate concern is that, far from subsiding, 
energy prices have again climbed sharply on fears of renewed Middle East escalation.  
The prices of gasoline, diesel, jet fuel, and European/Asian natural gas are now near 
or even above their March/April highs, with likely negative effects on consumers and 
energy-importing economies more broadly.

### Page 2

3. Although we don’t expect significant fiscal policy changes in 2027, the consequences 
of large-scale deficits—coupled with AI-linked borrowing and central bank hikes—have 
started to show up more clearly in higher longer-term interest rates.  The moves have 
been mostly orderly, but the increase since early 2026 averages a sizable 100bp across 
DM yield curves.  If sustained, we estimate that this increase will subtract a cumulative 
0.6% from GDP via weakness in housing, consumer durables, and business investment.  
(Only part of the drag is incorporated in our standing forecasts because we expect bond 
yields to pull back as central banks deliver fewer hikes than priced in markets.)

Exhibit 2: Oil Price Risks Are Skewed to the Upside

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research
26 October 2026   2
Goldman Sachs Global Views

### Page 3

4. The growth impulse from the AI boom was set to peak in late 2026 or early 2027 even 
before concerns around the sustainability of the AI investment boom intensified in 
recent weeks.  Although AI investment is still rising rapidly, we expect the increase as a 
share of GDP to slow over the next year.  Alongside the fading boost from last year’s 
increase in US depreciation allowances, this will weigh on capital spending growth, 
especially in the US.  AI has also boosted US consumer spending growth via a positive 
wealth effect worth an estimated 0.4pp (or 0.25% of GDP) over the past year.  This 
impact is likely to decline over the next year, gradually if stocks perform in line with our 
strategists’ reasonably upbeat expectations but more sharply if AI-related stocks 
continue to decline.

5. Our own base case remains that AI investment will ultimately pay off in terms of higher 
productivity and economywide profits.  However, one key channel for this payoff is labor 
market disruption, which has negative near-term macroeconomic effects.  And while DM 
labor markets still look solid in aggregate, we are starting to see clearer signs that the AI 
impact is spreading beyond the technology sector.  Across nonmanufacturing industries, 
the share of US firms that have adopted AI in regular business functions now shows a 
statistically significant negative correlation with employment growth and a statistically 
significant positive correlation with unemployment.

6. The Fed’s recent signals—explicit from Williams/Jefferson and implicit from Warsh—
effectively rule out a hike on October 28.  But the FOMC still looks very likely to deliver 
another 25bp hike to 4-4.25% on December 9 because spot inflation remains too high.  
And while we continue to expect significant disinflation next year on the back of 
reduced impulses from tariffs, energy prices, and AI-related prices, the risks to our 
forecast are on the upside, most obviously in energy.  Higher inflation could result in

another hike in March, but either way we see renewed cuts in late 2027/early 2028.

7. Like the Fed, the ECB will likely hold rates steady next week and deliver a final hike (to 
2.75%) at the December meeting.  However, the turbulence in the French bond market 
poses somewhat greater downside risks to that call than in the US.  The turbulence has 
tightened financial conditions to a degree that is likely to shave ¼-½pp from annualized 
growth in coming quarters.  And if it intensifies into December, markets might perceive a 
hike as signaling insufficient focus by the ECB on the risk of Euro area fragmentation.  
While the ECB could address this issue by coupling a hike with renewed balance sheet 
expansion, the hurdle for such a move is high, in part because purchases of French 
bonds would inevitably raise concerns about moral hazard with respect to the needed 
fiscal adjustment.

### Page 6

November 5.  Consistent with our view that the UK economy is stronger than widely 
perceived, GDP growth has surprised on the upside this year and the unemployment rate 
declined to 4.7% in August on a 3-month average basis (with the volatile 1-month rate 
plunging to 4.1%).  Rising inflation and the looming household energy price reset in 
January imply a significant risk of further moves, although market pricing of 4+ hikes 
(including November) still looks excessive.  We also expect hikes in Canada and New 
Zealand this week and in Australia next week.

9. Slightly faster than expected growth in Q3 has pushed up our 2026 GDP forecast for 
China to 4.6%.  But most activity indicators are much weaker.  Both property activity and 
fixed asset investment are contracting year-on-year, and while nominal retail sales 
growth picked up to +1.1% in September, large retailers (whose sales are relatively 
better measured) are still posting substantial year-on-year declines.  With domestic 
demand rising slowly at best, sustained GDP growth in the official 4.5-5% target range 
will require ongoing steep increases in the trade surplus.  But the surplus is already at 1% 
of global GDP, and further increases will eat more noticeably into output and 
employment abroad.  Ultimately, China’s trading partners may respond by imposing 
trade barriers that weigh on Chinese exports and GDP, although the measures taken so 
far have been limited.

Exhibit 8: UK Growth Forecasts Have Drifted Higher

Source: Bloomberg, Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research
26 October 2026   6
Goldman Sachs Global Views

### Page 7

10. Our strongest global view is that central banks will deliver fewer hikes than currently 
priced.  In addition, our rates strategists flag underweight positioning, diminished 
technical risks, and risk asset pressures as reasons why the recent rally might extend.  
The market’s narrow breadth makes our equity strategists wary of momentum factor 
exposure, but they still expect the S&P 500 to resume its climb after the midterm 
election, as earnings continue to grow solidly.  Our credit strategists remain cautious on 
IG technology bonds but see scope for some near-term reversal in HY given the scale of 
the recent selloff.  Our FX strategists expect the Japanese yen to act as a hedge against 
further AI jitters, in part because such jitters would shrink US-Japan interest rate 
differentials.  And our commodity strategists recommend targeted exposures to oil 
products and European natural gas to hedge near-term escalation risks alongside 
longer-term exposures to copper, gold, and select power markets to benefit from 
structural supply/demand imbalances. 
Jan Hatzius

• October CPI to rise 0.41% with the annual

up from the 0.6% increase in September.  
• October Trimmed Mean (TM) is set 
to gain 0.42%mth lifting the annual 
pace to 3.7%yr from 3.6%yr. 
• There is a small downside revision 
from our October 19th estimate for 
the December quarter CPI to 1.2%qtr 
while the TM remains 0.9%qtr.  
• The December quarter will represent the 
peak in Australian inflationary pressure. 
Our March quarter CPI remains 0.4%qtr 
and the TM is unchanged at 0.7%qtr. 
• We now see TM inflation dipping below 
3% by Q3 2027 and be well within the 
RBA’s target band by end 2027 at 2.7%yr.
• Demand remains the larger driver of 
inflation, but the recent pick-up in 
trimmed mean inflation appears to 
have been largely supply-driven.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Inflation accelerated in September...
The CPI rose 0.8% in the month of September, stronger than 
Westpac’s 0.6% estimate and the market’s expectation of 
0.7%. The annual pace picked up from 4.0%yr to 4.3%yr. The 
seasonally adjusted CPI rose 0.6% in September highlighting 
some positive seasonality in the month. 
Key factors driving the headline monthly result were: auto 
fuels (9.4%mth); domestic holiday travel & accommodation 
(8.0%mth); international holiday travel & accommodation 
(7.2%mth); and property rates & charges (5.5%mth). There 
were modest offsets from: women’s garments (–3.1%mth); 
household textiles (–6.6%mth); and accessories (–4.1%mth).
The more important underlying measure, the Trimmed Mean 
(TM), gained 0.3% in September, spot on both Westpac and 
market expectations with the annual pace lifting from 3.5%yr 
(revised from 3.6%yr) to 3.6%yr. The six-month annualised 
pace is now 4.0%yr the fastest in the short history of this 
series.
Overall, the breadth of inflation moderated for a second 
consecutive month. The share of the basket running above 
3%yr fell from 40% to 35%, while the share below 2%yr 
rose from 27% to 32%. Meanwhile, our replication of the San 
Francisco Fed’s inflation shock momentum index moved into 
positive territory after trending higher since April. Around 
14% of the CPI basket, on an expenditure-weighted basis, 
experienced positive inflation shocks in September, driven 
by holiday travel, fuel, and clothing & footwear. This was 
partly offset by negative shocks across 7% of the basket, 
concentrated in food & non-alcoholic beverages. For full 
analysis of the September CPI, see “ Inflation accelerating in 
the run up to Christmas”.
... but is set to peak in October.
Looking ahead to October, early indicators were mixed. The 
NAB business survey suggested firms continued to absorb 
a meaningful share of higher input costs, with purchase cost 
growth edging up to 2.4%qtr (+0.1ppt from September), while 
retail price growth slowed sharply to 0.2%qtr (–0.8ppts), 
partly reflecting some moderation in labour cost growth 
(see here). Meanwhile, consumers’ year-ahead inflation 
expectations rose to 5.3% in October, with this rate holding 
steady in November. Fuel prices provided a more pronounced 
source of inflationary pressure, with retail petrol and diesel 
prices jumping 8.7% and 5.3%, respectively, in October to 
average $2.40/l and $2.82/l as global oil prices approached a 
local peak.
October to mark the peak in inflation
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Neha Sharma, Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
We expect the October CPI to rise 0.4% (0.41% to 2dp), lifting
annual inflation to 4.8%, which we expect to mark the peak.
On a seasonally adjusted basis, we estimate a stronger 0.7%
increase (0.68% to 2dp). For the monthly trimmed mean, we

expect a 0.4% rise (0.42% to 2dp), nudging the annual pace up
to 3.7%. On a 3m/3m basis, the TM is tracking a 1.0% increase
in October. While conceptually different from the quarterly
trimmed mean, this measure nonetheless provides a useful
guide to near-term underlying inflation momentum. For market
services excluding volatiles & holiday travel, we have pencilled
in a 0.3% rise, lifting annual inflation to 4.0% from 3.8%,
which had marked a ten-month low.

Transport is expected to make the largest contribution
(+0.35ppts), almost entirely reflecting auto fuel (+0.32ppts).
Smaller contributions are expected from clothing & footwear
(+0.06ppts), household goods & services (+0.05ppts), and
meals out & takeaway (+0.04ppts). The main offset comes
from recreation & culture (–0.18ppts), driven entirely by
holiday travel (–0.18ppts), while fruit & vegetables, utilities
and several other components provide only marginal offsets.

Key details for the month of October
Food & non-alcoholic beverages are expected to rise 0.2% in
October, lifting annual inflation to 3.3%. Strength in the month
is concentrated in meals out & takeaway, where we expect
prices to rise 0.6%mth and 4.3%yr. We have allowed for a
greater-than-usual degree of price resetting as businesses
adjust to the card surcharging ban.
Housing inflation is expected to rise 0.1% in October, with the
annual pace increasing to 6.2% reflecting base effects from

electricity. Across the major components:
• Rents are expected to rise 0.2%mth and 3.5%yr, with the
late-September indexation of the Commonwealth Rent
Assistance flowing through more broadly in October. Our
seasonally adjusted estimate is stronger at 0.4%mth.
• New dwelling inflation has been a key source of downside
surprise in recent months. We expect a 0.2%mth rise
in October, which would see the annual pace moderate
to 4.5%. The downturn in established home prices has

deepened into year-end (see here), which should weigh on
new dwelling inflation over the months ahead. Our current
estimates point to a trough of around 1.3%yr by late-2027.
• Maintenance & repair costs are expected rise a solid
0.9%mth, taking annual inflation to 3.5%, while gas & other
household fuels provide the main offset, falling 2.5%mth.
Transport is expected to be the main source of upward
pressure in October, rising 2.9%mth and lifting to 11.6%yr.

This largely reflects an 8.2%mth jump in auto fuel prices
following the rise in global oil prices through the month. Motor
vehicle prices and spare parts are also expected to increase, by
0.4%mth and 1.6%mth respectively.

Recreation & culture is expected to provide the key offset, 
falling 1.5%mth, driven by a 3.0%mth decline in holiday travel. 
We also expect some pull-back across components exposed 
to the AI trade, including audio, visual & computing equipment 
(–0.2%mth) and games, toys & hobbies (–0.3%mth).
Underlying inflation holds momentum
As noted above, our October TM estimate is also 0.4%mth, a step 
up from 0.3% in September taking the annual pace to 3.7%yr from 
3.6%yr and lifting the six month annualised pace to 4.2%yr, from 
4.0%yr, the fastest pace in the short history of the series. Rents 
and dwellings continue to underpin the TM estimates. 
Upside risks to October baseline ...
We view the risks around the October CPI outcome as slightly 
tilted to the upside. Our estimates already allow for a greater 
degree of price resetting in areas such as meals out & takeaway 
and personal and household services, but there is a risk that this 
adjustment proves broader than we have assumed. Increases in 
fuel levy surcharges in early October are another source of upside 
risk, including increases by Woolworths and Australia Post, with 
the latter nearly doubling its surcharge between September and 
October. The key downside risk remains housing, particularly 
new dwelling prices, which have surprised to the downside in 
recent months and could continue to do so as the housing market 
downturn deepens. Trade notifications data also point to some 
further downside risk, with builders on average announcing 
smaller price changes than in prior months.
... but momentum to ease in November ...
We have a preliminary estimate for the November CPI of 
–0.1%mth which will see the annual pace ease back to 4.6%yr. 
Auto fuel moved lower through November to be down –2.1% in 
the month with seasonal falls in garments, health, furniture & 
household appliances and holiday travel offsetting some strength 
in food, housing, communication and financial services. 
Seasonally adjusted the CPI is estimated to lift 0.1% in November, 
highlighting the seasonal weakness in the month, while our TM 
estimate is for a 0.2% lift seeing the annual pace ease a touch to 
3.6%yr while the six month annualised pace drops from 4.2%yr 
to 3.9%yr. This moderation is expected to continue into December 
with a preliminary TM estimate of 0.2%mth/3.6%yr. 
The following page has a table with monthly profiles for the CPI, 
seasonally adjusted CPI and the TM out to July 2027. By July 
2027 we now expect an annual pace of 3.3%yr for the CPI and 
seasonally adjusted CPI while our estimate for the TM is 3.0%yr. 
More significantly, the six month annualised pace of the TM is 
estimated to be down to 2.7%yr by June 2027, well within the 
RBA’s target band. We have also included tables breaking down 
the monthly components of the CPI through the December and 
March quarters. 
... with December quarter now the peak ...
Our December quarter CPI estimate has been revised down 
slightly to 1.2%qtr, a modest 0.05ppt revision, with the annual 
pace lifting from 3.9%yr to 4.6%yr. The seasonally adjusted

CPI gains 1.4% in the December quarter highlighting normally
December is a seasonally soft quarter. Our TM estimate for
Q3 has a small downward revision of 0.05ppt to 0.85% at two
decimal place but this rounds up to our published estimate of
0.9%qtr. For now that suggests downside risk for our TM

estimate as getting the quarter to round to 0.8% is much
easier than getting it to round up to 1.0% . We also note
auto fuel prices falling faster than expected which may not
only result in downside risks to out CPI estimate but also our
TM estimate due to less pass through pressure.
... with inflation moderating through 2027 ...
Westpac’s preliminary March quarter CPI estimate is just 0.4%.
Driving most of this outcome is the 7.7% decline in autofuel

(–0.29ppt) and a 3.8% fall in holiday travel (–0.23ppt) being
offset by rising food (1.0%, 0.18ppt), housing (0.7%, 0.15ppt),
health (2.6%, 0.18ppt) and education (3.5%, 0.17ppt). The
March quarter does not have a lot in the way of net seasonally
with the seasonally adjusted estimate up 0.5%. Our underlying
TM measure of inflation is estimated to rise 0.7% in the March
quarter taking the annual pace down to 3.5% while the six
month annualised pace dips from 3.8%yr to 3.2%yr.

... as a disinflationary impulse kicks in.
We have for some time expected auto fuel prices to peak in
October, 240 cpl for petrol and 282 cpl for diesel, then to
trend lower from there. Our November estimates are currently
sitting on 237 cpl and 279 cpl respectively trending down to
186 cpl for petrol and 196 cpl for diesel by December 2027.
This represents a 12.7% fall in auto fuel through the first half
of 2027 subtracting 0.42ppt off the CPI over the same period

of time. For a detailed description on how we constructed the
auto fuels profile please see: “Persistent energy pressures
have further to run”. At this stage we have not adjusted our
Brent forecast in line with the current correction underway
(see the November Market Outlook for more details why) as
we expect this to be a volatile ride. Should the current correc-
tion continue, auto fuel will represent a larger drag on the CPI
and potentially the Trimmed Mean.

Critical to our expected moderation in core inflation is a step
down in dwelling price inflation on the back of falling estab-
lished home prices. From a 1.0%qtr increase in Q3 2026 we
see dwelling price increasing by 0.4% in Q4 2026, and then
0.5% in Q1 and 0.4%qtr in Q2 before dropping to 0.2%qtr in
Q3 and Q4. This will see dwelling price inflation in the CPI
drop from 5.4%yr at June 2026 to 2.3%yr by June 2027 then

down to 1.3%yr by December 2027.
At least to the end of this year there is still a high degree of
uncertainty about how much businesses will be able to pass
through upstream cost pressures. The latest NAB business sur-
vey continues to highlight robust input price pressure but also

that firms are struggling to pass on those increases.
For businesses, it is almost a now or never opportunity to raise
prices. We expect that through 2027, as energy prices ease
and the domestic economy slows under the weight of higher
interest rates and falling established home prices, households
will be much more resistant to price increases especially if dis-
cretionary household spending softens by more than expected.
That said, these headwinds may be at least partially offset by

the stronger starting point for household incomes, which has
proven more resilient than previously anticipated, and a possi-
ble bounce back in energy prices due to renewed conflict in the
Middle East.
Supply or demand driven inflation?
Inflation has steadily drifted away from the RBA’s target over
recent quarters. While the demand side is the larger driver of
inflation currently, most of the recent pick-up in TM inflation
appears to be from the supply side, and the more detailed

breakdown may understate how much.
There are several ways to split inflation into supply and de-
mand. One is to estimate how much of the rise a standard de-
mand-driven model can explain and attribute the rest to supply
(see here). Run through to Q3 2026, this approach puts most of
the recent pick-up in TM inflation down to supply. Without it,
trimmed mean would have risen from around 3.0%yr to 3.2%yr
over the year to Q3 2026, rather than the 2.9%yr to 3.7%yr

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
rise we actually saw. The supply contribution is moderate,
though, and well below its Covid-era peak. This analysis also
reveals that most of the disinflation through late-2023 and into
early-2025 reflected supply pressures unwinding, while de-
mand-driven inflation remained stickier, persisting above 3%yr.

Another is the San Francisco Fed approach, which the RBA has
also replicated (see here). The attribution is that if prices rise
and quantities fall, it is supply; if both rise, it is demand. We run
it to Q2 2026, as Q3 volume data are not yet available. Like the
first approach, it finds that demand accounts for most of infla-
tion (headline in this case). However, unlike the first approach,
it shows supply contributions easing a little.
A closer look at the categories classified as demand tells a

more nuanced story. Utilities, health and insurance all feature,
and their prices are largely shaped by government policy or are
not the categories that usually come to mind when people talk
about demand-driven inflation, and they are not very sensitive
to interest rates. You can look at electricity as a clear example
where prices in recent history have been set by government
policies (the introduction of, and then removal of, cost of living
rebates as well as policies to decarbonise the economy) or ex-

ternal supply shock (rising gas prices pushing up the marginal
cost of generation).
Categories more commonly thought of discretionary do show
up on the demand side, with recreation & culture (excluding
travel) and meals out & takeaway being the key ones. The
drawback of this sort of decomposition is that it labels cate-
gories where prices and quantities move together as demand,
even when this may reflect the ripple effects of a supply shock
elsewhere, a point our Chief Economist Luci Ellis detailed in a

note in 2024 (see In a world gone bananas, don’t squash the
other fruit). Some of this may be occurring now. In particular,
the analysis is likely not capturing the shift in spending towards
these categories resulting from travel disruptions associated
with the Middle East conflict (travel services itself was classi-
fied as a supply-side contributor in Q2). As travel normalises,
some of the “demand” contribution from recreation & culture
and meals out & takeaway should therefore fade over coming

quarters.
We would also acknowledge the work of Tim Toohey and his
analysis “The RBA has an identity problem”.

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.42 / 16 November 2026EDITORIAL
THE GLOBAL ECONOMY’S RESILIENCE TO SHOCKS: FOUR OBSERVATIONS AND THREE INTERTWINED QUESTIONS
A review of the latest economic indicators reveals four key observations. First, the global economy continues to show re-
silience in the face of the energy shock, which has recently been compounded by the impact of rising long-term interest
rates. Second, this resilience still relies heavily on the global investment cycle, but not exclusively: household consump-

tion also provides support. Albeit on a more or less strong footing depending on the country, its contribution to growth
is non-negligible. Third, the rise in inflation remains primarily energy-driven, with the United States standing out unfa-
vorably from the Eurozone due to a more entrenched and persistent problem. Fourth, the rise in long-term government
yields is placing a significant, well-identified, yet manageable strain on public finances. Three intertwined questions arise

regarding the rise in long-term interest rates: How high can they go? Will growth continue to hold up against all odds?
How will central banks react?
FIRST OBSERVATION: THE GLOBAL ECONOMY CONTINUES TO WEATHER THE
ENERGY SHOCK WITHOUT BREAKING
The surge in energy prices following the outbreak of the Middle East
conflict in late February has had a smaller impact on economic acti -
vity than the 2022 energy shock caused by the invasion of Ukraine 1.

Although the price of a barrel of Brent crude once again (and largely so)
crossed the USD 100 threshold in October while European natural gas
prices (TTF) remain under pressure as winter approaches, the macroe-
conomic shockwave has remained much more contained than four
years ago. According to business climate surveys in the Eurozone and
the United States, the manufacturing sector has weathered the shock
remarkably well, while the impact was more visible in the services

sector and on consumer confidence before an improvement set in.
In October, the overall picture remains positive: while there are signs of
erosion, they remain contained. The PMI/ISM indices remain above the
50-point expansion threshold — clearly so in the US, and to a slightly
lesser extent in Europe. Emerging economies have also shown resi -
lience, thereby fueling global growth. Non-resident portfolio invest -
ment flows have certainly been highly volatile since the outbreak of the

conflict in Iran, but financing conditions have held up despite pressures
on energy prices and interest rates in advanced economies, and the
aggregate manufacturing PMI index for the fifteen major emerging eco-
nomies has nearly returned to its February 2026 level since September.
SECOND OBSERVATION: THE SUPPORTIVE ROLE OF HOUSEHOLD CONSUMP-
TION, IN THE SHADOW OF THE INVESTMENT CYCLE
In the current business cycle, there is much more talk of corporate in-

vestment than of household consumption. The former is indeed riding
high, driven by several mutually reinforcing trends: the AI boom, in -
creased military spending, decarbonization and electrification efforts,
and Germany’s massive infrastructure plan. As a result, this invest -
ment engine appears immune — or at least less sensitive, at the aggre-
gate level — to the energy shock than household consumption. Inco -
me and financing constraints appear to be more severe for household

consumption. This is evident in household confidence, which certainly
does not reflect the same level of optimism as business climate indica-
tors. And yet, while they show little confidence, they are quite spend -
thrift, particularly in the United States. Across the Atlantic, the current
investment cycle is certainly a major driver of growth, closely trailing
that of household consumption. However, until the second quarter of
2026, household consumption retained its role as the primary driver, as

having the largest contribution to growth (see chart). And while it was
dethroned in the third quarter according to currently available data, its
support remains very much present and substantial.
Household consumption in the Eurozone certainly does not show the
same momentum: on a quarterly average, from the first quarter of
2023 through the second quarter of 2026, it grew nearly half as fast
as in the United States (0.34% vs. 0.66%). Nevertheless, it remains the

dominant driver of growth in the Eurozone (with a contribution amoun-
ting to 0.2pp, or 71%, compared with half a percentage point for total
investment), without the same wealth effect as in the United States
and without a decline in the savings rate, which leaves it with greater
potential for improvement.
THIRD OBSERVATION: THE RISE IN INFLATION REMAINS MOSTLY ENER-
GY-DRIVEN BUT APPEARS MORE ENTRENCHED ACROSS THE ATLANTIC
On the inflation front, the rise continues to be primarily energy-driven,

even though it appears more entrenched in the United States, and ini-
tial signs of pass-through — notably via producer prices — will be
closely monitored, while overall upward pressures remain strong 2.
In the Eurozone, where harmonised inflation reached 4.0% year-on-
year in October 2026, energy accounts for nearly half of the rise in
prices. Core inflation remains contained (at 2.6% y/y), as do all alter -
native indicators, including inflation expectations. The situation in the

United States, however, calls for increased vigilance. While headline
inflation (3.7% y/y in September according to the CPI) is lower than the

Eurozone October print, the underlying problem is more profound: core
inflation is picking up again, and nearly 60% of the components of the
US consumer price basket are rising by more than 3% (compared with
43% in the Eurozone).
FOURTH OBSERVATION: THE RISE IN LONG-TERM RATES ADDS A NEW, SIGNI-
FICANT BUT MANAGEABLE CONSTRAINT ON PUBLIC FINANCES
The return of 10-year sovereign yields to levels not seen in 25 years —

nearing 5% in the United States, in the United Kingdom, and in France
— brings back to the forefront a constraint that had previously faded:
debt service, while the state of public finances is generally worse today
than it was a quarter-century ago, with budgets under heavy strain
and facing difficult trade-offs3. Among the trends to watch, the spread
between the implicit interest rate on debt and nominal GDP growth
— which has long been favorable (i.e. negative) — is set to reverse

by the end of the decade in most European economies. It would re -
main negative but narrow significantly in Spain and the United States,
with Japan standing out as the exception, still enjoying a significantly
favorable spread. Another trend to watch is the magnitude of the in -
crease in the interest burden by 2032, which is forecasted to double,
for example, in France, Germany, and Japan, and reach 5% of GDP in
the United States.
Nevertheless, this additional constraint remains manageable in the

short and medium term thanks to two factors:
i/ the protective inertia of the average debt maturity when it is high, as
is the case in the Eurozone, Japan, and the United Kingdom;
ii/ the onset of gradual fiscal consolidation in most European countries
(except in Germany, which is implementing an investment stimulus
program).
The projected trajectories are, however, demanding and subject to po-
litical uncertainties. But at least there is a stated commitment to redu-

cing fiscal imbalances. This is not the case in the United States, where,
in addition, the relatively short average maturity of the debt exposes
the country to a faster pass-through of rate hikes to debt service.
THREE INTERTWINED QUESTIONS SURROUNDING THE RISE IN LONG-TERM
RATES
The rise in long-term sovereign rates has multiple causes, some of
which are positive (resilient growth, the AI boom, risk appetite). Fur -
thermore, for now, it remains broadly warranted and orderly. And as

long as this rise occurs alongside a rally in equity markets, overall
financial conditions are not particularly tightened as a result.
Since the rise in long-term rates is driven by multiple factors, we
should expect them to remain, at a minimum, at a high level or even to
rise further, even if some of the factors that have recently pushed them
higher begin to subside. According to our forecasts, the bulk of the rise
is likely behind us, as yields already factor in the resilience of growth

and residual monetary tightening. Long-term rates could then begin to
decline as fiscal consolidation gains credibility.
The recent turbulence in the tech sector has, however, shown that
what is gained on one front (reduced pressure on long-term rates as
safe-haven bonds regain favor) immediately creates another problem
(increased downside risk to economic activity from a reversal of the
AI boom). What matters for growth is the overall trend in monetary

and financial conditions, including access to bank credit. Recent data
point to first signs of tighter conditions, but from a favorable starting
point, and this tightening is helpful to dampen demand and curb asso-
ciated inflation. As long as it remains contained — which is the most
likely scenario at present — it should only moderately dampen growth
momentum without jeopardizing it. In France, for example, we will
continue to monitor this interplay between tailwinds and headwinds

through the cross-dynamics of business start-ups and bankruptcies4.
Nonetheless, the bulk of these questions presents a new conundrum
for central banks: to what extent should they accommodate the rise
in long-term rates, and to what extent does this rise interfere with
the conduct of monetary policy? Positively, by improving transmission?
But then, to what extent can they rely on this rise to do part of the
job without fueling the rise excessively (by tightening too much or too

little)? Or negatively, by leading to an excessive tightening of financial
conditions and access to credit? All of this argues in favor of the very
measured approach adopted so far by central banks, particularly the
Fed and the ECB. Further hikes in policy rates are to be expected, but
rather a few ones than a lot, in order to fine-tune and support the cur-
rent growth cycle for as long as possible.
Hélène Baudchon
helene.baudchon@bnpparibas.com

動態更新 租賃 ‧ 台灣

中租-KY 
(5871.TW/5871 TT)

3Q26延滯率再度上揚; 初步正向看待 2027F營運展望 
重要訊息 
中租 3Q26 稅後淨利季減 4%，主要資金成本上揚及呆帳提存增加，但 10
月獲利月增 2.5%至 17.3 億元，反映營收動能回升帶動營業利益改善。 
評論及分析 
資金成本上揚及東協預期信用損失回升帶動 3Q26 獲利季減 4%。中租
3Q26 稅後淨利 50.5 億元，季減 4%、年增 11%，低於凱基預估 5%，其中
營業利益季減 6%、年增 11%，主要利息費用及銷貨成本增加帶動營業成本
季增 6%，及預期信用損失季增 6%，抵銷利息收入季增 2%表現。分區來
看，3Q26 中國獲利季增 0.3%、年增 42%，顯示當地營運動能逐季回溫， 
相較台灣獲利因利息成本及營業費用增加而季減 3.5%，東協獲利則受柬埔
寨呆帳提存增加影響，季減 21%。中租前 10 月獲利年增率擴大至 6%，主
要預期信用損失及營業費用改善，帶動營業利益年增 10%。 
3Q26 整體延滯率上揚 0.1%至 5.2%，信用成本季增 11 基點。相較 3Q26
中國延滯率維持 6.9%，東協延滯率季減 0.2%至 4.3%，台灣延滯率季增
0.2%至 4.4%，主要反映沖銷呆帳減少，實際上新增延滯季減 4.7 億元。管
理層表示中國資產品質持續穩定，雖 3Q26 新增延滯仍季增 3.1 億元。中租
3Q26 利差季增 8 基點至 6.95%，主要中國利差季增 19 基點，抵銷台灣利
差季減 7 基點(資金成本上揚 6 基點及較低收益放款增加)。 
2027F 營運展望正向。3Q26 中租放款年增率減緩至 4%，其中台灣及中國
放款各年減 1%及 0.1%，管理層表示 YTD 放款 2%符合公司今年成長目
標。管理層此次尚未提供 2027F 放款成長目標，但考量中國資產品質穩定、
台灣資產品質維持平穩，加上油價對東協的影響逐步淡化，公司對 2027 年
營運採取正向看法；凱基推估 2027F 放款成長有機會高於 2026 年水準。管
理層表示 2026F 信用成本仍預期低於 3.8% 的先前目標，並預估 4Q26F 台
灣資金成本雖仍小幅上揚，但將持續提高新承做放款利率來維持利差水準。 
投資建議 
投資建議維持「增加持股」評等，目標價下調至 125 元，基於 2026F-27F
股價淨值比 1.1X 及 2026F-27F 本益比 10.5X，反映 2026F-27F 獲利預估各
下修 3.7% 及 2.5%。凱基預期中租近期股價表現將偏弱，反映 MSCI 台灣
及國泰台灣 ESG 永續高股息 ETF 基金 00878 移除其成分股，但預期 2026F 
現金股利殖利率超過 5.5%，對長期投資人仍具吸引力。 
投資風險 
台灣、中國及東協資產品質惡化高於預期。

圖 12: 公司概況  圖 13: 組織架構圖 
中租-KY (5871) 起源於 1977 年中國租賃(臺灣)，主要從事生產設
備融資業務，1995 年中國租賃與迪和公司合併並更名為中租迪
和。2005 年正式進軍中國，成立仲利國際租賃。2009 年成立中
租控股，營業據點已遍及台灣、中國、泰國、越南、馬來西亞、
柬埔寨、菲律賓及美國等地。旗下子公司包括中租迪和、仲利國
際、和迪、仲信資融等。主要營業項目為租賃、分期付款、應收
帳款受讓級直接融資。1H26 業務別營收佔比分別為：利息收入
77%、銷貨收入 9%、手續費收入 5%、租賃收入 7%及其他
2%；地區別獲利佔比分別為：台灣 55%、中國 36%、東協
9%。

### Page 6

台灣 中租-KY

十一月 12, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R 6

ESG 圖表定義 
項目 定義 說明 
能源使用 
直接與間接能源消耗總量(十億焦耳) 
- 公司運營範圍內消耗之能源總量 
- 能源使用總量 = 直接能源消耗總量 + 間接能源消耗量 
- 總能源使用量包括購買的能源、生產的能源 
- 就公用事業而言，輸電/電網損耗為其業務活動之一部分，被視為總能源消耗，數據不包括
為滿足能源使用而生產的電力（公用事業為出售而生產） 
- 就公用事業而言，用於能源生產的煤、天然氣或核能等原料不屬於「總能源使用」項下

購買之再生能源 
Primary 再生能源購買總量(十億焦耳) 
- 公司各種來源之能源消耗量與購買的能源中屬於自然界可再生者（太陽能、風能、水能、
生物質能、地熱能）之量  
- 如無證據顯示再生能源由公司生產，則所報告的能源數據視為購買的再生能源

再生能源使用率 再生能源占總能源消耗量之比例  
CO2 約當排放量 
直接 CO2 與 CO2 約當排放量(公噸) 
- 公司擁有或控制的來源的直接排放量（範圍 1 排放量） 
- 相關氣體：二氧化碳 (CO2)、甲烷 (CH4)、一氧化二氮 (N2O)、氫氟碳化物 (HFCS)、全氟化合
物 (PFCS)、六氟化硫 (SF6)、三氟化氮 (NF3)

CO2 約當排放量營收比 
直接 CO2 與 CO2 約當排放量（公噸）銷售（百萬元）比 
- 公司擁有或控制的來源的直接排放量（範圍 1 排放量） 
- 相關氣體：二氧化碳 (CO2)、甲烷 (CH4)、一氧化二氮 (N2O)、氫氟碳化物 (HFCS)、全氟化合
物 (PFCS)、六氟化硫 (SF6)、三氟化氮 (NF3)

廢棄物總量 
廢棄物總量(公噸) 
- 總廢棄物 = 無害廢棄物 + 有害廢棄物 
- 僅考慮固體廢棄物，但如液體廢棄物以公噸為單位呈報，則會將其加入求得包含液體廢棄
物之總量 
- 對於採礦、石油與天然氣等行業，tailings、廢石、煤、飛灰等廢棄物亦考慮在內

廢棄物回收率 
公司呈報的廢棄物回收率 
- 廢棄物回收率 = 廢棄物回收量/總廢棄物*100 
- 廢棄物轉化為能源或經由廢棄物焚燒產生能源視為廢棄物回收 
- 經由堆肥回收的廢棄物視為回收之廢棄物

總取水量 
總取水量(立方公尺) 
- 由呈報組織直接或經水公司等中介機構從任何水源抽取的總水量 
- 水井、城鎮/公用事業/市政用水、河水、地表水等不同之水源均予以考慮

環保支出 
環保支出總金額 
- 所有用於環境保護，或防止、減少、控制環境因素、影響、危害的投資與支出，亦包括處
置、處理、衛生、清理支出

員工流動率 
員工流動率 
- 包括任何原因（自願或非自願）離開公司的員工，如辭職、退休、自然離職/死亡、醫療失
能、冗員、裁員、重組、解僱、裁減或定期合約到期 
- 員工流動率 =（離開之員工/平均員工人數）*100 
- 平均員工人數 =（本年末員工人數 + 去年末員工人數）/2 
- 本財年末員工數 =上個財年末員工數 + 新員工數 - 離開之員工數

女性經理 
女性經理百分比 
- 女性經理占公司經理的百分比 
- 如有不同階層之百分比，如最高階、高階、中階、初階，則會考慮中階女性經理之百分比 
- 女性經理百分比 = 女性經理人數/經理總數*100 
女性主管比例逐年上升，2020 年
來到 27.9%。 
女性員工 
女性員工百分比 
- 女性員工占公司員工總數的百分比 
- 女性員工百分比 = 女性人數/員工總數*100 
2020 年集團男性員工比例 
51.1%，女性員工比例 44.9% 
教育訓練總時數 
所有員工教育訓練總時數 
- 僅考慮員工教育訓練時數 
- 包括一般員工所有類型的教育訓練（如健康與安全、環境、急難事件救援、技能與職業發
展） 
- 如果數據以天為單位，則乘以 8，係假設 1 天 =  8 小時工作

每位員工教育訓練時數 平均每年每位員工總教育訓練時數  
資料來源：Refinitiv；凱基

法說會議重點摘要

博智 
(8155 TT, NT$255, 未評等)

重點摘要 
1. 3Q26毛利率季增 3 ppts 至 26.9%，來自於高階產品出貨比重拉升，
漲價效應。 
2. 2027 年展望，AI Server 增加、 IPC: B/B >1、IC Tester: 機種、客戶增
加。 
3. 2028 年 AI Server 需求持續強勁， IC Tester 比例增加，低軌衛星與太
空 AI 伺服器專案，博智具備極高機會參與高階板材製造。 
結論 
我們認為公司在 AI 伺服器與高毛利 IC Tester 比重提升下將大幅受惠，目
前股價交易於 2027 市場共識之每股盈餘的 11.3 倍。 
營運概況 
 1Q-3Q26 累計合併營收達 48 億元，較 2025 年同期成長 35%。
2026 年單季營收呈逐季增長，累計 1Q-3Q26 年增達三成以上。累
計每股盈餘達到 11.15 元。3Q26 毛利率跳升，因高階產品出貨比
重拉升，且成功將上游 CCL 材料漲價順利轉嫁給客戶。 
 2026 產品結構，Server 營收占比 82%、IC tester 9% 、IPC 7%及
Networking 1%。 
 2026 板層數營收佔比：14-20L、 22-34+L、 2-12L 以上佔比為
50%、27%、23%。 
 博智核心技術：  
 實際量產中非對稱堆疊（N+M）設計顯著增加，極易引發板彎
翹。 
 需在高層數、大尺寸及板彎翹條件下精準控制背鑽深度，加工數
千至數萬個不同深度之孔位。公司具備高重橫比電鍍及 Multi-
layer 集成能力。 
 博智核心 SI（訊號完整性）模擬實力：，CCL 材料交期拉長
時，博智憑藉強大的 SI 模擬能力，能協助客戶快速尋找並驗證 
Second source 備用材料，大幅降低認證門檻與缺料衝擊。 
 目標市場現況：  
 Server：主流聚焦約 8-30 層板，排版尺寸最大達 22x27 吋，板
厚不超過 5mm，採用 M6-7 材料。受限於現有產能，優先配置
生產非高階 HDI 之 N+M 及 2/3 次壓合高層板。 
 IPC：應用於工業、醫療與機器人，層數 2-26 層，尺寸 20x24 
吋，板厚約 3.0mm，採用 M4-6 材料。(訂單能見度:6-12 個月
訂單)。

 IC Test：包含 Load board（LB）、 Burn In Board、SLT(System-
level test) 探針卡，層數達 6-48 層，最大排版尺寸 24x28 吋，
板厚達 6.4mm，採用 M4-6 材料。 
 未來 2-3 年技術趨勢：  
 Server：層數向>30L， 18+18、20+24 等高難度堆疊推進，排
版尺寸擴大至 24x30 吋，板厚 6.5mm，伺服器主板大舉融合 
N+M 與 HDI 設計。(訂單能見度:6-8 個月訂單)。 
 IC Test：出現 50 層以上需求，板厚規劃拉升至 10mm，兩次至
三次壓合疊構顯著增加。(訂單能見度:3-6 個月訂單) 
 上游原物料交期現況，主要合作夥伴為台灣三大 CCL 廠 
 E-glass（E-布）交期，最長達 16 至 20 週。 Low-loss 布（K-
布）交期極為誇張，最長達 8-12 週。 
 與 CCL 供應商協商調配不同廠區之產能支援。與客戶定期更新
交期資訊，客戶配合下長訂單，並同步建立替代材料驗證。 
 2027-28 年產品線展望  
AI Server 需求持續強勁，一般 Server 保持穩健。IC Test 隨 2Q27
越南廠量產開出，台灣廠將騰出產能全力擴大 IC Test 業務，客戶
數與機種將大幅增加。2028 低軌衛星與太空 AI 伺服器專案， 結合
大股東仁寶在衛星地端與天線端之佈局。目前已有將 AI 伺服器推
向太空 AI 伺服器實際專案進行中，博智具備極高機會參與高階板
材製造。 
 ASP 與毛利率展望 
 伺服器平台（Intel 18 層、AMD 20-30+ 層）層數大幅升級，帶
動單價與毛利率走揚。 
 高重橫比（Aspect Ratio）與 IC Test 技術門檻：  
 AI 伺服器重橫比達 15-16 以上，IC Test 更高達 20-30 以上，市
售標準設備無法保證，需仰賴博智獨家製程設計。 
 越南廠（ACM）相較東南亞同業之優勢，泰國 PCB 同業正面臨嚴
重技術人員缺口。博智攜手仁寶合資，建廠僅耗時 9 個月，目前已
在地聘僱並由仁寶越南廠代訓人員，進度順利。 
 美國大選後對 Data Center 搶地、搶電之政策管控趨嚴，可能使建
置節奏稍微放緩，但 AI 總體需求強勁，長遠發展更趨健康。AI 產
業榮景估計仍有 10 至 15 年長路。

Document 626: 宏观点评：如何理解10月经济的三个变化？

Source PDF: all report pdf/宏观点评：如何理解10月经济的三个变化？.pdf
Report source: 东吴证券
Date: 2026-11-16
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

证券研究报告·宏观报告·宏观点评

东吴证券研究所

2026年11月16日

证券分析师  芦哲 
执业证书：S0600524110003 
 luzhe@dwzq.com.cn 
证券分析师  占烁 
执业证书：S0600524120005 
 zhansh@dwzq.com.cn

相关研究  
《二手房销售延续回升，新房销售降
幅有所收窄》 
2026-11-15 
《外部紧缩冲击、国内承接整固，结
构转向两端平衡》 
2026-11-15

[Table_Tag]    [Table_Summary] 
◼ 10月经济运行有所波动，生产和消费增速回落，投资降幅扩大，部分指
标受到节日错位、生产节奏变化和政策传导时滞等因素影响。生产端，
10月规模以上工业增加值同比增长4.8%，较9月回落0.7个百分点；
服务业生产指数同比增长4.6%，较9月小幅回落0.2个百分点。需求
端，10月社会消费品零售总额同比增长0.4%，回落0.7个百分点；固
定资产投资同比下降8.1%，降幅较1-9月扩大0.9个百分点。其中，广
义基建投资、制造业投资和房地产开发投资分别下降 7.3%、2.9%和
21.6%，降幅分别较1-9月扩大1.2、0.8和1.0个百分点。 
◼ 如何理解工业生产、消费、投资三个领域的边际变化？工业生产强度下
降主要与内需边际走弱有关，另外，汽车生产强度降幅较大，可能与行
业贸易协定带来的预期变化有关，国内外汽车市场现实需求在10月波
动不大。消费主要是通讯器材、金银珠宝以及必需消费品增速波动较大，
通讯器材是由于大量需求提前到9月释放，导致短期波动加大；金银珠
宝则是金价下降开始影响到同比增速；必需消费品受到中秋假期错位、
价格下跌等因素影响。投资降幅扩大与去年的趋势一致，去年 9-10月
陆续落地“两个五千亿”政策后，投资降幅在四季度并未收窄，但在今
年一季度明显回升，这表明从政策出台、资金落地到形成实物工作量存
在一定时滞。 
◼ 往后看，消费节奏仍有望逐步恢复，政策资金向投资的传导值得期待，
经济动能有望企稳回升。消费方面，耐用品整体降幅收窄仍然是消费最
重要的支撑，随着中秋错月、食品价格等因素减退，消费有望趋于稳定。
投资方面，考虑到传导滞后的历史经验，一季度有望快速修复。生产方
面，高出口的现实仍将对年内工业生产形成支撑。 
◼ 一、生产端：工业生产增速回落，新动能保持韧性 
◼ 10月工业生产增速有所回落。10月规模以上工业增加值同比增长4.8%，
较9月回落0.7个百分点；季调环比增长0.21%，较9月回落0.40个百
分点。三大门类增速均有所回落，公用事业回落幅度相对较大。10月制
造业增加值同比增长5.4%，较 9月回落0.6个百分点；采矿业增长0.7%，
回落0.6个百分点；电力、热力、燃气及水生产和供应业增长4.4%，回
落1.2个百分点。 
◼ 分行业看，汽车和黑色金属增速回落，高技术制造业继续发挥支撑作用。
10月汽车制造业增加值同比增长0.9%，较9月回落5.8个百分点；黑
色金属冶炼和压延业同比下降1.7%，9月为增长1.6%。与此同时，高
技术制造业增加值同比增长17.6%，较 9月加快2.3个百分点；计算机、
通信和其他电子设备制造业增长19.4%，加快3.0个百分点。 
◼ 汽车制造业增加值下降对工业整体影响较大。按照2023年投入产出表，
汽车制造业占工业增加值的5.4%计算，回落5.8个点，对应拖累0.3个
点的整体工业增加值。 
◼ 但是无论从国内来看，还是从海外来看，10月的汽车市场都相对稳定。
从国内来看，社零中的汽车零售同比下降21.4%，与9月的 -21.2% 差
别不大。从海外看，汽车出口增速也仍然维持高位，10月汽车（包括底
盘）出口同比增长37.6%，相比9月增速有所下降，但更多是基数变化
带来的。整体看，10月国内外汽车需求都相对平稳。 
◼ 因此，影响汽车制造业生产强度的，可能更多是预期的因素，而非现实
因素。10月 8-9日，中欧举行贸易投资磋商，就混动汽车贸易达成谅
解。企业对欧洲市场的出口预期和排产安排可能随之调整。短期仍需关
注对欧订单、出口发运及零部件生产的变化。 
◼ 服务业生产保持增长。10月服务业生产指数同比增长4.6%，较9月回
落0.2个百分点；1-10月累计增长4.7%，与1-9月持平。

### Page 2

2 / 5 
◼ 二、消费端：节日错位扰动食品烟酒，后续消费有望修复 
◼ 10月社零增速有所回落，主要受到通讯器材、金银珠宝和必需消费品
增速波动的影响。10月社会消费品零售总额同比增长0.4%，较9月回
落0.7个百分点。以两年平均增速观察，7-9月社零分别增长2.1%、1.9%
和2.0%，连续三个月稳定在2%左右；10月回落至1.6%，低于此前的
运行水平。从商品结构看，通讯器材、金银珠宝以及食品、饮料、烟酒
等必需消费品增速回落较为明显，分别受到新品销售节奏、金价变化和
中秋错月等因素影响。 
◼ 通讯器材增速明显回落，主要受到新品上市带动需求提前释放以及去年
同期基数抬升的影响。10月通讯器材零售额同比增长6.5%，较9月回
落24.6个百分点。今年9月手机新品集中发布，带动通讯器材零售额
同比增长31.1%，部分需求在9月释放；与此同时，去年同期增速从9
月的16.2%升至10月的23.2%，也加大了10月同比增速的波动。 
◼ 金银珠宝零售额降幅扩大，金价回落开始影响名义销售增速。10 月限
额以上金银珠宝零售额同比下降28.7%，降幅较9月扩大19.1个百分
点。根据金价测算，10月金价同比上涨1.1%，较9月的17.8%明显回
落，为2023年3月以来最低增速。国家统计局数据显示，10月黄金饰
品价格同比上涨4.5%，涨幅较9月回落18.0个百分点。金银珠宝零售
额按名义金额统计，金价同比涨幅回落会削弱其销售增速；这一影响主
要来自黄金价格变化，不宜直接等同于居民消费内生动能的变化。黄金
价格的变动对金银珠宝同比消费的影响，在明年一季度之后会减弱。 
◼ 必需消费品增速回落，受到中秋错月和食品价格下降的共同影响。10月
限额以上粮油食品、饮料和烟酒零售额同比分别增长 0.5%、0.3%和
2.5%，较9月分别回落6.2、4.7和11.3个百分点。去年中秋为10月6
日，与国庆假期合并；今年中秋为9月25日，部分节日采购提前至9
月，抬高了9月相关商品的增速，也使10月同比比较基数相对偏高。 
◼ 2018年曾出现相似的节日错位，食品、饮料和烟酒增速在10月均明显
回落。2017年中秋为10月4日，与国庆假期合并；2018年中秋提前至
9月24日。受此影响，2018年限额以上粮油食品零售额同比增速从9
月的13.6%降至10月的7.7%，回落5.9个百分点；饮料从10.4%降至
7.9%，回落2.5个百分点；烟酒从9.8%降至1.2%，回落8.6个百分点。
国家统计局当时也指出，中秋假日前移是2018年10月社零增速回落的
主要影响因素之一。因此，今年10月必需消费品增速回落，需要结合
节日消费跨月变化来观察。 
◼ 三、投资端：10月投资有所调整，政策效果有望逐步显现 
◼ 10月投资降幅扩大，房地产、基建和制造业投资均有所回落。根据累计
数据推算当月同比，10月固定资产投资当月同比下降约16.1%，较 9月
扩大约8.9个百分点；其中，房地产开发投资下降约33.4%，广义基建
投资下降约17.6%，制造业投资下降约9.3%。从季调环比看，固定资产
投资由9月增长0.85%转为10月下降2.93%，9月投资改善并未延续。 
◼ 对基建投资而言，政策资金已经投放，向项目施工和投资数据的传导仍
需时间。截至10月28日，今年8000亿元新型政策性金融工具已全部
完成投放，加上5500亿结存地方债中部分用于项目建设资金，10月正
处于从资金拨付到实物工作量形成的过渡期。 
◼ 2025年的经验具有参考价值，当年四季度5000亿元新型政策性金融工
具完成投放，同时安排使用5000亿元地方政府债务结存限额。但2025
年四季度投资累计同比从9月-0.5%降至12月-3.8%，至2026年初才转
负为正。这表明资金从投放、配套融资到项目施工和形成实物工作量存
在时滞，今年四季度未必能看到投资回升，明年一季度概率更大。 
◼ 风险提示：房地产市场后续变化存在不确定性；投资止跌回稳存在反复
风险；地缘政治变化影响物价和外贸。

东吴证券投资评级标准 
投资评级基于分析师对报告发布日后 6至 12个月内行业或公司回报潜力相对基准表现
的预期（A 股市场基准为沪深 300 指数，香港市场基准为恒生指数，美国市场基准为标普 
500 指数，新三板基准指数为三板成指（针对协议转让标的）或三板做市指数（针对做市转
让标的），北交所基准指数为北证 50指数），具体如下： 
公司投资评级： 
买入：预期未来 6个月个股涨跌幅相对基准在 15%以上； 
增持：预期未来 6个月个股涨跌幅相对基准介于 5%与 15%之间； 
中性：预期未来 6个月个股涨跌幅相对基准介于-5%与 5%之间； 
减持：预期未来 6个月个股涨跌幅相对基准介于-15%与-5%之间； 
卖出：预期未来 6个月个股涨跌幅相对基准在-15%以下。 
行业投资评级： 
增持： 预期未来 6个月内，行业指数相对强于基准 5%以上； 
中性： 预期未来 6个月内，行业指数相对基准-5%与 5%； 
减持： 预期未来 6个月内，行业指数相对弱于基准 5%以上。 
我们在此提醒您，不同证券研究机构采用不同的评级术语及评级标准。我们采用的是相
对评级体系，表示投资的相对比重建议。投资者买入或者卖出证券的决定应当充分考虑自身
特定状况，如具体投资目的、财务状况以及特定需求等，并完整理解和使用本报告内容，不
应视本报告为做出投资决策的唯一因素。

Document 664: 日銀『経済・物価情勢の展望（2026年10月）』BOX3（高齢者消費分析）に対する構造的検証と評価 〜分析テーマ：ビッグデータ利用にお_849c4a1e

Source PDF: all report pdf/日銀『経済・物価情勢の展望（2026年10月）』BOX3（高齢者消費分析）に対する構造的検証と評価 〜分析テーマ：ビッグデータ利用にお_849c4a1e.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-11-06
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

Economic Trends

2026 年 11 月６日

1/4 
日銀『経済・物価情勢の展望（2026 年 10 月）』BOX3（高
齢者消費分析）に対する構造的検証と評価 
〜分析テーマ：ビッグデータ利用における選択バイアスと公的統計との比較分析～ 
 経済調査部 首席エコノミスト 永濱 利廣 
（℡：050-5474-7492）

（要旨） 
⚫ 問題意識: 日本銀行のレポート（2026 年10 月BOX3）は、クレジットカードデータ（JCB 消費 NOW）
をもとに「年金の目減りによって高齢者層の消費が減少し、小売業に影響を与えている」として
いるが、これにはデータ特性や論理展開に大きな課題があると考える。 
⚫ 主な統計的課題: 
1. 決済手段のバイアス: 現金決済の多い高齢者層において、クレカ限定のデータでは実態を正しく
捉えられない選択バイアスがある。 
2. 公的統計との乖離: 総務省の『家計調査』では高齢層の消費は横ばいであり、むしろ 30〜50 代
の消費低迷が目立っており、日銀の主張と矛盾する。 
3. 因果関係の短絡化: 売上不振を「年金の低下」へ直接結びつけるロジックは飛躍があり、極端な
社会保障論議を誘発する危険性がある。 
⚫ 特定のビッグデータは即応性がある一方で、属性によるバイアスがかかりやすいため、高齢層の
実態把握には『家計調査』などの公的統計と組み合わせた多角的な検証が不可欠である。

１．はじめに 
日本銀行が『経済・物価情勢の展望（2026 年10 月）』BOX3において提示した「年金目減りによる高齢
者層の消費減退および小売り業態への影響」という論旨は、直感的な説得力を持つ一方で、用いられた
データ特性と論理展開に重大な統計的課題を抱えている。特にクレジットカード決済データ（JCB 消費
NOW）は、クレジットカード取引データを基に、現金を含む消費全体の動向を推計した指数とされてお
り、家計の消費支出全体を直接観測していない。決済手段の利用状況が年代によって異なり、その変化
も生じる中で、高齢者層の消費動向をどこまで正確に捉えられるかについては、慎重な検証が必要であ
ろう。 
そこで本稿では、総務省『家計調査』などの公的統計との乖離を解明し、政策的論議への影響を含め
て客観的な検証を行う。

### Page 2

2/4 
２.日銀 BOX3 における論旨と背景 
日本銀行は2026年10 月の展望レポートBOX3 において、物価上昇下で個人消費が伸び悩む主要因の
一つとして「高齢者・年金受給者層の生活防衛意識（節約志向）の強まり」を指摘した。日銀が描く
因果のロジックは以下の通りである。 
⚫ 実質年金支給額の低下：実質年金支給額がパート・一般労働者の実質賃金よりも大きく下落して
いる。物価上昇に対し、マクロ経済スライド等の給付抑制メカニズムが機能することで、実質年
金支給額が急速に低下している（図表B3-2）。 
⚫ 高齢者層の実質消費減少：JCB 消費NOW データによれば、70 歳以上（破線）や 60～69 歳（緑線）
が 2021/1Q 比で顕著に下落。『JCB 消費NOW』の年齢階層別データによれば、39 歳以下などの若年
層が堅調であるのに対し、60 代・70 代以上の実質消費は 2021 年以降、右肩下がりで下落してい
る（図表B3-3）。 
⚫ 小売業態への波及：既存店ベースでは、百貨店が高水準を保つ一方、スーパー・コンビニは足元
で頭打ち・下落傾向。この高齢者の需要減退が、スーパーマーケットやコンビニエンスストアに
おける売上数量の抑圧要因になっている（図表 B3-6）。

（出所）日銀展望レポート（2026 年 10 月）

３．BOX3 分析における3 つの懸念点と統計的課題 
① 決済手段のバイアス（JCB消費 NOWの標本選択性） 
『JCB消費NOW』は速報性と詳細な属性分析に優れた民間ビッグデータであるが、その購買データは
「クレジットカード決済」に限られる。日本における高齢層（特に 70 代以上）は、日常的な食料品や
日用品の購入（スーパー・コンビニ・ドラッグストア等）において現金決済の比率が依然として圧倒

### Page 3

3/4 
的に高い。クレカ利用頻度や決済能力が高い特定の高齢層のみを抽出したデータをもって「高齢者全
体の消費減退」と推論することは、選択バイアス（Selection Bias）を引き起こしている。 
② 総務省『家計調査』との顕著な乖離 
公的統計での代表である総務省『家計調査』（世帯主の年代別実質消費支出・季節調整値）の推移
を観察すると、日銀が提示した JCBデータの結論とは大きく異なる状況が浮かび上がる。

『家計調査』データによれば、2021年 1Q 以降の実質消費支出において、最も力強い推移を見せてい
るのは単身・若年層（〜29 歳）であり、70代以上（水色）および 60代（緑色）はほぼ100の横這い
圏内で踏みとどまっている。一方で、住宅ローンや教育費負担が重く、物価高の影響を直撃している
30 代・40代・50 代の実質消費支出こそが継続的に低迷（90〜95 水準へ落ち込み）している。これ
は、「高齢者ほど消費が右肩下がりで減少している」という日銀BOX3 と相反する事実である。 
③ 因果関係の過度な単純化と政策論議悪用のリスク 
BOX3では「マクロ経済スライドによる実質年金低下」→「高齢者の消費減退」→「コンビニ・スー
パーの売上不振」という一連のストーリーが描かれている。しかし、業態別の売上不振には、単身化
の進行、商品価格の値上げに対する客数減少、立地動向、人口動態変化（現役世代の行動様式変化）
など多角的な構造要因が複雑に絡み合っている。 
原因を「年金改定による購買力低下」へと短絡的に結びつける論旨は、実証分析として飛躍がある
だけでなく、年金支給額やマクロ経済スライドに関する政治的・財政的な論争に悪用される危険性を
はらんでいると言えよう。

### Page 4

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
4/4 
４.評価軸の対比と客観的解釈 
日銀BOX3の整理と、客観的な実証分析に基づいた解釈の違いを以下に比較整理する。 
評価軸 日銀BOX3 の整理 客観的・実証的な解釈 
主要データ JCB 消費 NOW（クレカ決済データ） 家計調査・商業動態統計等の公的統計も併用すべき

（出所）各種資料より筆者作成

５．結論と今後の分析に向けた提言 
民間ビッグデータである『JCB消費NOW』は、高頻度かつ即応的なトレンド把握において非常に有用
なデータである。しかし、属性フィルター（クレジットカード利用者という限定された層）による選
択バイアスを考慮せずに、全世代の代表値として扱うことには統計的リスクが伴う。 
中央銀行の公式レポートにおいては、高頻度民間データの有用性を認めつつも、現金決済比率の高
い高齢者層の実態を捉えるために『家計調査』などの公的統計とのマルチデータ検証（トライアンギ
ュレーション）が不可欠である。特定のデータが示す極端な傾向のみに基づいて年金制度やマクロ消
費動向の判断を下すことは避け、客観的かつ多角的な検証姿勢を維持することが求められる。

摘要：
特朗普 9 ⽉在英伟达股票上的买卖操作，因政策利益冲突问题再度引发外界关注。
据周四公开的最新财务披露⽂件，美国总统特朗普 9 ⽉买⼊总价最⾼达 26.5 万美元的英伟达股
票，同期卖出最⾼ 1.5 万美元。联邦官员在财务披露表格中以区间形式申报资产价值。这份披露
由特朗普于 11 ⽉ 1 ⽇签署，由美国政府伦理办公室对外发布。
与此同时，特朗普政府正就英伟达先进芯⽚出⼝政策展开讨论，相关决定将直接影响这家芯⽚
巨头的业务前景。
这⼀买⼊动作发⽣在⻩仁勋婚外情丑闻曝光前， 9 ⽉英伟达股价运⾏在 210 ⾄ 230 区间，⽬前已经
跌⾄ 150 。特朗普本⼈表⽰，对于有关⻩仁勋的相关指控，他感到 " 震惊 " 。

美国国会议员已就特朗普对⻩仁勋个⼈情况的了解程度展开询问。《纽约时报》报道称，特朗普
曾于 2025 年获得简报，内容涉及⻩仁勋婚外情，但他选择不就此采取⾏动。
特朗普 9 ⽉在英伟达股票上的操作，只是当⽉数百笔交易之⼀。据路透社此前报道， 8 ⽉特朗普
已买⼊最⾼ 160 万美元的英伟达股票，并卖出最⾼ 100 万美元。
同⼀份披露⽂件显⽰，特朗普 9 ⽉还买⼊最⾼ 31.5 万美元的 SpaceX 股票。SpaceX 持有价值数⼗
亿美元的政府合同，其业务⾛势与联邦机构审批⾼度挂钩。特朗普在本届任期内多次交易
SpaceX 股票，包括在 2026 年 6 ⽉和 7 ⽉。

王毅送别美俄元⾸后举⾏记者会。他表⽰，本次 APEC 经济体领导⼈会议达成深圳展望，这

11 ⽉ 19 ⽇，中共中央政治局委员、外交部⻓王毅举⾏中外记者会。王毅表⽰，我刚刚在机场相
继送别了特朗普总统和普京总统，所以让⼤家在这久等了，各位⾟苦了。这⼏天⼤家积极地报
道这次 APEC 会议，我也代表主办⽅向各位记者表⽰我们衷⼼的感谢。
王毅：会议达成深圳展望，这是 APEC 中国年最重要的成果
11 ⽉ 19 ⽇，中共中央政治局委员、外交部⻓王毅举⾏中外记者会。王毅表⽰，当今国际局势动
荡不安，世界经济增⻓承压，逆全球化不断抬头， 21 个 APEC 经济体的领导⼈和代表⻬聚深圳，

共同探讨应对之策，共同协商合作⼤计，不仅对开创亚太合作新局具有重⼤意义，也将为维护
世界和平与发展产⽣深远影响。这⼀刻世界的⽬光投向中国，聚焦深圳。
在习近平主席引领下，在各个经济体领导⼈的⽀持下，会议达成了富有雄⼼、务实全⾯的亚太
经合组织领导⼈深圳展望，这是 APEC 中国年最重要的成果。
王毅：这是⼀次和平峰会
11 ⽉ 19 ⽇，中共中央政治局委员、外交部⻓王毅举⾏中外记者会。
王毅表⽰，今天下午，习近平主席同普京总统、特朗普总统举⾏了会晤，就事关全球和平安全
与稳定的重要问题深⼊交换了意⻅，这是三国元⾸历史上⾸次举⾏会晤，意义重⼤，影响深
远，是载⼊史册的标志性事件，会晤⽓氛坦诚友好，富有建设性，取得了⼀系列的共识，对外
发布了⾯向世界的和平宣⾔，核⼼就是共同维护世界和平，促进⼤国关系战略稳定，可以说这
是⼀次和平峰会。
王毅：中俄美元⾸会晤为新时期⼤国协调互动构建了新范式
11 ⽉ 19 ⽇，中共中央政治局委员、外交部⻓王毅举⾏中外记者会。
谈及中俄美元⾸会晤，王毅表⽰，这是⼀次具有开创意义的历史性的会晤。⼤国关系牵动国际

格局的演变，事关全球战略问题。三国元⾸历史上⾸次举⾏这⼀⾯对⾯的会晤，开创了三国最
⾼领导⼈交往的新纪录，也为新时期的⼤国协调互动构建了新范式。
王毅：中俄美元⾸⾯对⾯介绍各⾃战略意图，同意要防⽌发⽣冲突对抗
11 ⽉ 19 ⽇，中共中央政治局委员、外交部⻓王毅举⾏中外记者会。

營收走勢圖

研究員/莊佳蓉 EVA8894122@uni-psg.com

研究部維持鴻海買進投資建議，主係：(1)3Q26 營收、營益率及 EPS
皆創單季新高，亦優於預期；(2)管理層將 26 年全年展望全面上修，
能見度較 8 月法說會更佳；(3) VR 機櫃自動化程度提升，預期產線爬
坡速度較快；(4) 27 年AI 機櫃出貨可維持高雙位數成長，且NeoCloud
客戶持續擴增，VR 整體市佔率將高於 GB；(5)零組件垂直整合有助提
升獲利品質。綜上所述，研究部 27 年營收微幅上修至 14.89 兆元（原
估 14.58 兆元），EPS 微幅上修至 24.79 元（原估 24.23 元）。考量美
國資料中心建置審批趨嚴，給予 12 倍本益比，目標價下修至 300 元，
維持買進建議。

重點摘要： 
1. 3Q26營收、獲利創單季新高，全面優於預期 
3Q26營收3.03兆元（原估2.89兆元），季增19.8%，年增47.0%，
營收達成率104.8%，優於預期，主係AI伺服器需求強勁，雲端
網路成長優於管理層原先樂觀預期，消費智能及元件亦季增顯
著。毛利率6.0%（原估5.7%），季減0.1pts，優於預期，季減
主係產品組合變化及原物料價格上漲。營利率3.8%（原估
3.3%），季增0.1pts，規模擴大帶動費用率下滑，營運槓桿持
續發酵。業外收益34億元，低於原估54億元。EPS5.51元（原
估4.73元），優於預期。

2. 4Q26四大產品全面季增，上修營收及獲利預估 
展望4Q26，根據管理層指引：(1)雲端網路，新一代AI伺服器
機櫃開始出貨，AI機櫃出貨預期季增高雙位數，通用伺服器溫
和成長，季增顯著、年增強勁；(2)消費智能，進入出貨高峰，
需求穩健且產品售價提高，季增及年增皆強勁；(3)電腦終端，
受記憶體供應緊張影響，季增略衰退，年增顯著；(4)元件及
其他，季增顯著。綜上所述，研究部上修4Q26營收至3.52兆元
（原估3.26兆元），季增16.3%，年增35.0%，毛利率5.8%、營
利率3.4%皆與原估相同，EPS上修至5.96元（原估5.60元）。

管理層將2026年全年展望全面上修：消費智能由顯著成長上修
為強勁成長、電腦終端由衰退上修為顯著成長、元件及其他由
持平上修為顯著成長，三大產品線都比前次法說好，而雲端網
路維持強勁成長。

### Page 2

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  2

3. VR機櫃自動化程度提升，預期產線爬坡速度較快 
根據研究部調查，VR200在4Q26僅小量出貨，真正大量須等到
1Q27底~2Q27，但VR機櫃因compute tray採用模組化設計，自
動化程度顯著提高，預期學習曲線爬坡快。另據研究部調查，
鴻海在VR接單能力良好，市佔持續增加。展望2027年，主要CSP
仍持續擴大AI投資，管理層預期AI機櫃出貨可維持高雙位數成
長，大型CSP仍 為 最 主 要客 戶 ， 此 外 ，27年 也 將 加入 更 多
NeoCloud客戶。針對資料中心建置及AI放緩議題，管理層表示，
終端算力需求依然強勁，目前未見放緩跡象。

4. 垂直整合支撐獲利品質 
ASIC方 面 ， 鴻 海 與 主 要CSP皆 有 合 作 ， 然ASIC專 案 多 數 採
Consign交易模式，營收占比相對較低。垂直整合方面，鴻海
已具備從零組件到CPO交換機的完整能力，並持續提高零組件
自製比重。管理層預期26年營利率將優於25年的3.2%，中長期
營利率3%以上的目標不變。

5. 訂單動能未見下修，維持買進建議 
綜上所述，研究部上修2026年營收至11.19兆元（原估10.79兆
元），年增38.1%，毛利率6.0%（原估5.9%），營利率3.6%（原
估3.5%）， EPS上修至19.31元（原估18.17元）。2027年營收微
幅上修至14.89兆元（原估14.58兆元），年增33.1%，毛利率5.4%
（原估5.3%），營利率3.3%（原估3.2%）， EPS微幅上修至24.79
元（原估24.23元），年增28.4%。考量美國資料中心建置審批
趨嚴，研究部給予12倍本益比，目標價由327元下修至300元，
維持買進建議。

著 作 權 ， 嚴 禁 抄 襲 與 仿 造 。
